}\quad\text{with}\quad\beta^{(2)d}=\frac{2A+B}{2(16\kappa k)^{2}}c_{G}^{2}\dim G\,.

(79)

By applying the same rules as for
\beta^{(2)E}_{a\bar{b}}
, we obtain

A=-y^{4}\,,\quad B=\frac{1}{2}y^{4}-\frac{2}{3}xy^{3}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\beta^{(2)d}=\frac{(1-\kappa^{2})^{3}(3+13\kappa^{2})}{3072(\kappa k)^{2}}c_{G}^{2}\dim G\,.

(80)

Fixed points of the RG-flow give rise to CFTs. Their central charge is related to the value of
\beta^{d}
as
Metsaev:1987zx

c=6\beta^{d}\,.

(81)

Taking into account that
\beta^{(0)d}=D/6
, where
D
denotes the dimension of the target space, we extract for the fixed point at
\lambda=0
the central charge

c=\dim G\left(1-(2k)^{-1}c_{G}\right)+\mathcal{O}(k^{-3})

(82)

by combining (
52
) and (
80
). Matching it with the central charge of the level
\widehat{k}
WZW-model on the Lie group
G

Knizhnik:1984nr
,

c=\frac{2\widehat{k}\dim G}{2\widehat{k}+c_{G}}\,,

(83)

we see that
k=\widehat{k}+1/2c_{G}
. Again this observation is in agreement with equation (3.8) of
Georgiou:2019nbz
.

2.4
Finite generalised Green-Schwarz transformation

From a conceptual point of view, finite gGS transformations are straightforward. They just exponentiate the infinitesimal version
\delta_{\lambda}
. Formally, this was already done in (
43
) but at the end of the day, one needs an explicit prescription how this transformation acts on the metric,
B
-field, and dilaton. This is more complicated than one might initially think because it requires an infinite tower of
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrections. As we restrict our discussion to
\beta
-functions up to two loops, we can fortunately circumvent this problem and just need to compute the first contribution. More precisely, we consider

e^{\delta_{\lambda}}\widehat{E}_{A}{}^{I}\widehat{E}_{BI}=\Lambda_{A}{}^{C}\Lambda_{B}{}^{D}(\Lambda_{CD}+\Delta_{\Lambda}\widehat{E}_{CD})

(84)

with

\Delta_{\Lambda}\widehat{E}_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\Delta_{\Lambda}g_{a\bar{b}}+\Delta_{\Lambda}B_{a\bar{b}}\\
-\Delta_{\Lambda}g_{b\bar{a}}+\Delta_{\Lambda}B_{b\bar{a}}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(85)

To evaluate the scheme transformation (
67
) that links
\widehat{\upbeta}
with
\upbeta
, one has to compute
\Delta_{\Lambda}^{(1)}g_{a\bar{b}}
and
\Delta_{\Lambda}^{(1)}B_{a\bar{b}}
, respectively. There are slightly different ways how one can do this
Borsato:2020bqo
;
Hassler:2020tvz
. Of course all of them lead to the same result
Borsato:2020bqo

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Lambda}^{(1)}g_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\Theta_{(i|\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}\omega^{(-)}_{|j)\bar{b}}{}^{\bar{a}}+\frac{1}{4}\Theta_{i\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}\Theta_{j\bar{b}}{}^{\bar{a}}

(86)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Lambda}^{(1)}B_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\Theta_{[i|\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}\omega^{(-)}_{|j]\bar{b}}{}^{\bar{a}}+B^{\Theta}_{ij}

with

\Theta_{i\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}=\partial_{i}\Lambda^{\bar{c}}{}_{\bar{a}}\Lambda_{\bar{c}}{}^{\bar{b}}\,,\qquad\omega^{(-)}_{i\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}=\omega_{i\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}-\frac{1}{2}H_{i\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}\,,

(87)

and

\mathrm{d}B^{\Theta}=-\frac{1}{12}\Theta_{i\bar{a}}{}^{\bar{b}}\Theta_{j\bar{b}}{}^{\bar{c}}\Theta_{k\bar{c}}{}^{\bar{a}}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{j}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{k}\,.

(88)

In order to keep these equations as simple as possible, we frequently switch between flat and curved indices by contracting with the frame
e_{a}{}^{i}=e_{\bar{a}}{}^{i}
from (
10
) or its inverse transpose
e^{a}{}_{i}=e^{\bar{a}}{}_{i}
. Not surprisingly, the resulting field redefinitions are still quite complicated and cumbersome even for simple, low dimensional examples. Hence, one should rather perform all calculations in the adopted scheme of
\upbeta
. Let us revisit the simplest
\lambda
-deformation on SU(2)
Sfetsos:2013wia
to emphasise this claim.

2.4.1
SU(2)
\lambda
-deformation

Generators in the fundamental representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2)
can be conveniently written in terms of the three Pauli matrices
\sigma_{a}
as

t_{a}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{a}\,.

(89)

Note that we use an exotic normalisation that results in
c_{\mathrm{SU(2)}}=4
rather than the standard value of
2
. It will become obvious shortly that this choice is required to match with the results in
Hoare:2019mcc
. With the group element

g=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}-i\alpha\cos\gamma\sin\beta&-\alpha(\cos\beta-i\sin\beta\sin\gamma)\\
\alpha(\cos\beta+i\sin\beta\sin\gamma)&\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}+i\alpha\cos\gamma\sin\beta\end{pmatrix}\,,

(90)

we obtain the leading order metric,
H
-flux and dilaton,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{\kappa(1-\alpha^{2})}\mathrm{d}\alpha^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}\kappa k}{\Delta}\mathrm{d}s^{2}(S^{2})

(91)

\displaystyle H^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{k\alpha^{2}\left[2\kappa^{2}+(1-\kappa^{2})\Delta\right]}{\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}\Delta^{2}}\mathrm{d}\alpha\wedge\mathrm{vol}(S^{2})

\displaystyle\phi^{(0)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log\Delta

\displaystyle\quad\text{with}\quad\Delta

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}+\alpha^{2}(1-\kappa^{2})\,,

after implementing the discussion in section
2.1.1
. They match equation (3.10) in
Hoare:2019mcc
and fix the normalisation (
89
) we use for the generators
t_{a}
. In order to make the expression more readable, we use the round-two sphere
S^{2}
with the metric
\mathrm{d}s^{2}(S^{2})=\mathrm{d}\beta^{2}+\sin\beta\mathrm{d}\gamma^{2}
and the volume form
\mathrm{vol}(S^{2})=\sin\beta\mathrm{d}\beta\wedge\mathrm{d}\gamma
as a reference. Evaluating (
23
) for (
25
) and (
28
), one obtains the one-loop
\beta
-function for the metric and
B
-field, which can be written as

\widehat{\beta}^{(1)E}_{ij}=-c_{G}\frac{(\kappa^{2}-1)^{2}}{8k\kappa}\Lambda_{ji}\,,

(92)

where
\Lambda_{ij}
is the curved version of
\Lambda_{\bar{a}\bar{b}}
in (
18
). As expected, this equation agrees with (
44
). Another remarkable property, which is not directly obvious at the level of the target space fields, is

\frac{\mathrm{d}\left(g_{ij}^{(0)}+B_{ij}^{(0)}\right)}{\mathrm{d}\kappa}=-\frac{1}{\kappa}\Lambda_{ji}\,.

(93)

It can be used to verify the
\beta
-function for
\kappa
in (
50
) and emphasise that already the one-loop computations involving
\widehat{\beta}
are more opaque than the ones for
\beta
. In the same vein, one checks the
\beta
-function of the generalised dilaton.

Using (
86
), we evaluate the corrections to the metric and
B
-field,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Lambda}^{(1)}\mathrm{d}s^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\kappa^{2})\Delta+2\kappa^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}\left[(1-\alpha^{2})^{-1}\mathrm{d}\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{2}\mathrm{d}s^{2}(S^{2})\right]

(94)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Lambda}^{(1)}B

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{2}\kappa\sin\beta}{\Delta^{2}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}}}\left[4\gamma\mathrm{d}\alpha\wedge\mathrm{d}\beta+2\alpha(\alpha^{2}-1)(\kappa^{2}-1)\mathrm{d}\beta\wedge\mathrm{d}\gamma\right]\,,

which originate from the finite gGS transformation with the parameter
\Lambda_{\bar{a}\bar{b}}
. Combining them with the scheme transformation (
60
), we obtain the
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrections

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2(1)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8\kappa^{4}-8\kappa^{2}(1-\kappa^{2})\Delta-3(1-\kappa^{2})^{2}\Delta^{2}}{4\kappa k\Delta^{2}}ds^{2(0)}

(95)

\displaystyle H^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{4}\left[12+\Delta(3\Delta-14)\right]+2\kappa^{2}(3-2\Delta)\Delta+\Delta^{2}}{k\left[\kappa^{2}(2-\Delta)+\Delta\right]\Delta^{2}}\kappa H^{(0)}

\displaystyle d^{(1)}

\displaystyle=0\,.

As a check, one can evaluate the two-loop
\beta
-function (
183
)
4
4

4

Equation (
183
) does not include the infinitesimal diffeomorphism
\xi^{(1)i}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})
from (
122
) which generates the second term in (
59
). Thus, we add it to get the
\widehat{\beta}^{(2)}
in (
96
).
for the generalised dilaton for this corrected target space geometry. With the help of the xCoba Mathematica package, we find

\widehat{\beta}^{(2)d}=\frac{(1-\kappa^{2})^{3}(3+13\kappa^{2})}{3072(\kappa k)^{2}}c_{G}^{2}\dim G\,,

(96)

which matches (
80
). We could continue to compute the
\beta
-functions of the metric and
B
-field. For them, performing the scheme transformation is more involved. Moreover, one has to account for a further correction from a partial double Lorentz frame fixing, as explained in section
3.2
. Because it will not provided any further insights, we will not present this complicated calculation here.

3
Doubled gradient flow

The results in the last section are self-contained and can be used without additional insights into how they were obtained. Still, it is of course interesting to see how we systematically derive expressions like (
37
) and (
69
). Thus, we will go step by step through the derivation in the following.

A crucial observation is that it is in general highly complicated to compute O(
D
,
D
)-covariant
\beta
-functions directly. To avoid this problem, we exploit the fact that they alternatively arise from a gradient flow,

\delta_{\Psi}S=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\Psi\cdot K(\upbeta)\,,

(97)

where
K(\upbeta)
is an invertible rank two tensor on the coupling space. In order to obtain
\upbeta
, it is sufficient to know
S
and
K
. Splitting (
97
) order by order in
\alpha^{\prime}
, one finds

\displaystyle\delta_{\Psi}S^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\Psi\cdot K^{(0)}(\upbeta^{(1)})

(98)

\displaystyle\delta_{\Psi}S^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\Psi\cdot\left[K^{(0)}(\upbeta^{(2)})+K^{(1)}(\upbeta^{(1)})\right]\,.

Because
K^{(0)}
does not contain any derivatives, it is just a matrix and can be inverted easily. With the inverse, which is fixed completely by a one-loop computation, it is straightforward to extract
\upbeta^{(1)}
and
\upbeta^{(2)}
. At a first glance, this route might seem more complicated than just trying to directly rewrite the known results for one and two-loop
\beta
-functions of the bosonic string in a doubled, O(
D
,
D
)-covariant way. However, we will see that it is much easier. Especially, since the covariant expressions for
S^{(1)}
and
S^{(2)}
are already known
Marques:2015vua
. Furthermore,
K^{(0)}
follows nearly immediately from known results in DFT. Hence, the remaining challenge is to find
K^{(1)}
and bring it in an O(
D
,
D
)-covariant form. In doing so, a considerable advantage is that
K^{(1)}
just contains two derivatives and dealing with Bianchi identities simplifies significantly compared to
S^{(2)}
or
\upbeta^{(2)}
.

We start with the one-loop computation in the next subsection. It contains all the major ingredients of the gradient flow (
97
) in a simple setting. After introducing all required quantities, we demonstrate how the
\beta
-functions from section
2.2
arise. Subsequently, we address the two-loop
\beta
-functions in section
3.2
. They require to additionally discuss scheme transformations, partial double Lorentz gauge fixing, and gGS transformations. Manifest PL symmetry does not only simplifies the
\beta
-functions considerably but also
K
, which governs the gradient flow. Hence, we explain in section
3.3
how one computes the
c
-function and the corresponding gradient flow metric of PL
\sigma
-model. In the spirit of section
2
, we discuss the
\lambda
-deformation as an explicit example.

3.1
One-loop

The starting point of our derivation is the one-loop
\beta
-functions
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)E}_{ij}
and
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)\phi}
from section
2.2
. For convenience, we decompose the former into its metric and
B
-field contribution,
\widehat{\beta}^{E}_{ij}=\widehat{\beta}^{g}_{ij}+\widehat{\beta}^{B}_{ij}
. Hence, the three
\beta
-functions

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{ij}

\displaystyle=R_{ij}-\frac{1}{4}H^{2}_{ij}+2\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\phi\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{l}H^{l}{}_{ij}+\nabla_{l}\phi H^{l}{}_{ij}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(1)\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}\phi-\frac{1}{24}H^{2}+(\nabla\phi)^{2}

(99)

form the basis of our discussion. Each line contains an infinitesimal diffeomorphism with
\xi^{(1)i}=\nabla^{i}\phi
, which relates the respective
\widehat{\beta}
-function to
\overline{\beta}^{(1)g}_{ij}
,
\overline{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ij}
and
\overline{\beta}^{(1)\phi}
.

In order to understand how these
\beta
-functions arise from a gradient flow, we vary the one-loop effective target space action,

\widehat{S}^{(1)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}e^{-2\phi}\left(R-\frac{1}{12}H^{2}-4(\nabla\phi)^{2}+4\nabla^{2}\phi\right)\,,

(100)

of the bosonic string. By comparing the result

\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(1)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\left(-\delta g^{ij}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{ij}-\delta B^{ij}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ij}+8\delta d\widehat{\beta}^{(1)d}\right)

(101)

with the first equation in (
98
), we verify that the
\beta
-functions (
99
) indeed arise from a gradient flow. Like before, we use the
\beta
-function for the generalised dilaton (
38
) instead of
\widehat{\beta}^{\phi}
. Moreover, we can easily read off
K^{(0)}(\widehat{\upbeta})
. For the following discussion it is crucial that the first two terms in the integral come both with a minus sign. For the metric, this sign is subtle as we can either vary with respect to the metric or its inverse (both differ by a sign). All metric variations we perform are with respect to the metric
g_{ij}
. Thus, the natural index position for
\delta g
is
\delta g_{ij}
and the corresponding
\beta
-function has both indices raised. Due to the superscripts
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}
is carrying, it is usually more convenient to use exactly the opposite notation, like in (
101
). This is perfectly fine, as long as we keep in mind that the variation is still with respect to the metric and not its inverse.

Our next objective is to rewrite (
101
) in terms of the O(
D
,
D
)-covariant quantities from section
2.1
. To this end, we first obtain the variation of the generalised frame
\widehat{E}_{A}{}^{I}
,

\delta\widehat{E}_{A}{}^{I}\widehat{E}_{BI}=\delta\widehat{E}_{AB}+\delta\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{gf}}_{AB}

(102)

with

\delta\widehat{E}_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta g_{a\bar{b}}+\delta B_{a\bar{b}}\\
-\delta g_{b\bar{a}}+\delta B_{b\bar{a}}&0\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{gf}}_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\delta B_{ab}&0\\
0&\delta B_{\bar{a}\bar{b}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(103)

\delta g_{ab}
and
\delta B_{ab}
denote the flattened variations of the metric and
B
-field (
\delta g_{ab}=e_{a}{}^{i}e_{b}{}^{j}\delta g_{ij}
and
\delta B_{ab}=e_{a}{}^{i}e_{b}{}^{j}\delta B_{ij}
). All fluctuations of the generalised frame field in (
102
) split into two parts because
\widehat{E}_{A}{}^{I}
is partially gauge fixed to a distinguished double Lorentz frame. If we would only apply
\delta\widehat{E}_{AB}
, whose form is identical to the
\widehat{\upbeta}_{AB}
in (
29
), we would destroy this gauge fixing. Hence, we have to additionally apply the compensating gauge transformation
\delta\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{gf}}_{AB}
. The same pattern applies to the
\beta
-function and we readily define

\widehat{\upbeta}^{E\mathrm{gf}}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\beta}^{B}_{ab}&0\\
0&\widehat{\beta}^{B}_{\bar{a}\bar{b}}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(104)

Because the one-loop action
\widehat{S}^{(1)}
is invariant under double Lorentz transformations, gauge fixing terms drop out from the doubled version,

\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(1)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\left(\delta\widehat{E}^{AB}K^{(0)}_{AB}{}^{CD}\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)E}_{AB}+8\delta d\widehat{\beta}^{(1)d}\right)\,,

(105)

of (
101
). But they will become relevant at two loops, as we discuss in section
3.2.3
. From (
105
), we read off
K^{(0)}
for the metric and the
B
-field. It takes the surprisingly simple form

K^{(0)}_{ABCD}=2\eta_{AC}\eta_{BD}\,.

(106)

In DFT, the action
\widehat{S}^{(1)}
is expressed in terms of the generalised Ricci scalar,

\widehat{S}^{(1)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}\,.

(107)

There are two different ways to write
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}
, either in terms of a generalised metric or generalised fluxes. We adopt the latter, the flux formulation
Siegel:1993th
;
Hohm:2010xe
;
Geissbuhler:2013uka
, where it reads

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}=P^{AB}P^{CD}\left(\overline{P}^{EF}+\frac{1}{3}P^{EF}\right)\widehat{F}_{ACE}\widehat{F}_{BDF}+2P^{AB}(2\widehat{D}_{A}\widehat{F}_{B}-\widehat{F}_{A}\widehat{F}_{B})\,.

(108)

Finally, we compute the variation
5
5

5

The variations are exactly the ones given in (
53
). Furthermore, performing integration by parts with

\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\widehat{D}_{A}\cdot=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\widehat{F}_{A}

(109)

is required.
of this action with respect to
\delta\widehat{E}_{AB}
and
\delta d
,

\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(1)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\left(\delta\widehat{E}^{AB}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{AB}^{(1)}-2\delta d\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}\right)

(110)

with

\mathcal{G}^{(1)}_{AB}=4P_{[A}{}^{C}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{D}\left(\widehat{F}_{CEG}\widehat{F}_{DFH}P^{EF}\overline{P}^{GH}+\widehat{F}_{CDE}\widehat{F}_{E}P^{EF}+\widehat{D}_{D}\widehat{F}_{C}-\widehat{D}_{E}\widehat{F}_{CDF}\overline{P}^{EF}\right)\,,

(111)

and compare the result with (
105
). One directly reads off
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)E}_{a\bar{b}}=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}_{a\bar{b}}
,
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)d}=-\frac{1}{4}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(1)}
and thereby obtains the results discussed in section
2.2
.

3.2
Two loops

Beyond one-loop,
\beta
-functions become scheme dependent and we have to choose a scheme to start with. There are two popular options for the bosonic string, the Metsaev-Tseytlin
Metsaev:1987zx
(MT) scheme and the Hull-Townsend (HT) scheme. Both are connected by a scheme transformation which is detailed in appendix
B
. We found it a little easier to extract
K^{(1)}
from the results presented in
Metsaev:1987zx
and therefore we start from the two-loop
\beta
-functions in the MT scheme
6
6

6

Note that
H^{2}_{ab}=H_{acd}H_{b}{}^{cd}
,
H^{4}_{ab}=H_{acd}H^{cef}H_{eg}{}^{d}H^{g}{}_{bf}
and that the signs of the last two terms in the first line of (
113
) are flipped compared to
Metsaev:1987zx
. It seems that there happened a misprint in
Metsaev:1987zx
, because the combination of the signs in (
113
) is the one which arises from the variation of the target space effective action in appendix
A
. It is also required to obtain the
B
-field
\beta
-function in the HT scheme after the appropriate scheme transformation (see appendix
B
for details).

Metsaev:1987zx

\displaystyle\overline{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[R_{acde}R_{b}{}^{cde}+\frac{1}{8}(H^{4})_{ab}+\frac{3}{4}\nabla_{c}H_{ade}\nabla^{c}H_{b}{}^{de}+\frac{1}{8}H^{cd}{}_{a}H_{db}{}^{e}{}(H^{2})_{ce}

(112)

\displaystyle\qquad-R^{cdef}H_{acd}H_{bef}-\frac{5}{2}R_{(a}{}^{cde}H_{b)cf}{}H_{dbe}{}^{f}-\frac{1}{2}R^{c}{}_{ab}{}^{d}(H^{2})_{cd}+\frac{1}{12}\nabla_{a}H_{cde}\nabla_{b}H^{cde}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\overline{\beta}^{(2)B}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\Big[2R_{[a|cde}\nabla^{c}H_{|b]}{}^{de}+\nabla^{c}H_{de[a}H_{b]}{}^{fd}H_{cf}{}^{e}+2\nabla_{c}(H^{2})_{d[a}H_{b]}{}^{dc}-\frac{1}{2}H^{2}_{cd}\nabla^{c}H^{d}{}_{ab}\Big]\,,

(113)

\displaystyle\overline{\beta}^{(2)\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\Big[R_{abcd}R^{abcd}+\frac{5}{24}H^{4}+\frac{4}{3}\nabla_{d}H_{abc}\nabla^{d}H^{abc}+\frac{3}{8}H_{ab}^{2}(H^{2})^{ab}

(114)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{11}{2}R^{abcd}H_{abe}H_{cd}{}^{e}-2H^{2}_{ab}\nabla^{a}\nabla^{b}\phi\Big]\,.

Note that we use flat indices because this is more in line with the objects we expect to find in the O(
D
,
D
)-covariant rewriting we are looking for. But as the covariant derivative
\nabla_{i}
annihilates by construction the frame field
e_{a}{}^{i}
, which is used to go from flat to curved indices, switching between the two becomes just a relabeling. Like we have seen in the last subsection, instead of
\overline{\upbeta}
, the gradient flow usually involves a different member in the same equivalence class,
\widehat{\upbeta}
, which is obtained by an infinitesimal diffeomorphism and/or a
B
-field gauge transformation. More precisely, we take
\xi^{(2)i}=-\frac{1}{48}\nabla^{i}H^{2}
and
\chi^{(2)}_{i}=0
in (
23
) to fix
\widehat{\beta}^{(2)E}_{ij}
and
\widehat{\beta}^{(2)\phi}
respectively.

After a cumbersome computation, which is summarised in appendix
A
, we find that the variation of the two-loop target space effective action

\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\frac{1}{4}\Big[R_{abcd}R^{abcd}-\frac{1}{2}R^{abcd}H_{abe}H_{cd}{}^{e}+\frac{1}{24}H^{4}-\frac{1}{8}(H^{2}_{ab})^{2}\Big]

(115)

gives rise to

\displaystyle\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\Bigr[

\displaystyle-\delta g^{ab}\widehat{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}-\delta B^{ab}\widehat{\beta}^{(2)B}_{ab}+8\delta d\beta^{(2)d}+\widehat{K}^{(1)d}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})

(116)

\displaystyle+\delta g^{ab}\widehat{K}^{(1)g}_{ab}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g},\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})+\delta B^{ab}\widehat{K}^{(1)B}_{ab}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g},\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})\Bigl]

with

\displaystyle\widehat{K}^{(1)d}(\beta^{B})

\displaystyle=H^{abc}\nabla_{a}\beta^{B}_{bc}\,,

(117)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}^{(1)g}_{ab}(\beta^{g},\beta^{B})

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{2}\beta^{g}_{ab}+\nabla^{c}\nabla^{\vphantom{g}}_{(a|}\beta^{g}_{c|b)}-\frac{3}{4}H_{acd}H_{be}{}^{d}(\beta^{g})^{ec}-\frac{1}{4}H^{2}_{(a|c}(\beta^{g})_{|b)}{}^{c}

(118)

\displaystyle\quad-2\left(\nabla_{(a|}\beta^{g}_{cb)}-\nabla_{c}\beta^{g}_{ab}\right)\nabla^{c}\phi+H^{cd}{}_{(a|}\nabla_{c}\beta^{B}_{d|b)}+\frac{1}{2}H^{cd}{}_{(a|}\nabla_{|b)}\beta^{B}_{cd}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{(a}H_{b)cd}\beta^{Bcd}+\beta^{B}_{ca}\beta^{Bc}{}_{b}\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{K}^{(1)B}_{ab}(\beta^{g},\beta^{B})

\displaystyle=-H_{[a|}{}^{cd}\nabla_{c}\beta^{g}_{d|b]}-\frac{1}{2}R_{ab}{}^{cd}\beta^{B}_{cd}-\frac{1}{4}H_{abc}H^{dec}\beta^{B}_{de}+\frac{1}{4}H_{acd}H_{be}{}^{c}\beta^{Bed}

(119)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}H_{de[a|}H^{dec}\beta^{B}_{c|b]}\,.

It is actually rather non-trivial to bring
\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(2)}
into this form. That it is still possible demonstrates the power of the gradient flow equations (
98
).

3.2.1
Physically equivalent choices for
\widehat{K}^{(1)}

The expressions (
117
) to (
119
), we obtained for
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
, cannot be brought into a doubled, O(
D
,
D
)-covariant form as given. However, they can be modified to overcome this problem. More specifically there are at least four different ways to change an arbitrary
\widehat{K}
while keeping the physics it describes unchanged:

1)

Assume that the
\beta
-functions are shifted by a combination of an infinitesimal gauge transformation and diffeomorphism which is parameterised by
\Xi^{I}=\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{i}&\xi^{i}\end{pmatrix}
and mediated by the generalised Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}
,

\widehat{\upbeta}\rightarrow\widehat{\upbeta}+\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}\widehat{\upbeta}\,.

(120)

Moreover, take
\Xi
to be a function of the one-loop
\beta
-functions, which contains one additional derivative. If we want to keep the second gradient flow equation in (
98
) invariant, we have to adapt
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
according to

\widehat{K}^{(1)}\rightarrow\widehat{K}^{(1)}-K^{(0)}\mathcal{L}_{\Xi^{(1)}}\,.

(121)

We will do exactly this with

\xi^{(1)i}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})=\frac{1}{4}H^{ijk}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ij}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi^{(1)}_{i}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})=\frac{1}{2}\omega_{i}{}^{ab}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ab}\,.

(122)

2)

Additionally, the invariance of the action
\widehat{S}
under generalised diffeomorphisms gives rise to relations between
\beta
-functions. In particular, one can use

\delta_{\mathcal{L}_{\Xi}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{E}&d\end{pmatrix}}\widehat{S}^{(1)}=0

(123)

to obtain the identities

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}{}_{ab}\nabla^{b}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{b}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}{}_{ab}+\frac{1}{4}H_{a}{}^{bc}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{bc}-\nabla_{a}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)d}

(124)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\nabla^{b}\Bigl(e^{-2\phi}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ab}\Bigr)\,.

3)

Shifting
\widehat{K}^{(1)B}_{ab}(\beta^{g},\beta^{B})
by

\frac{1}{2}\left(\widehat{\beta}^{(0)B}_{c[a}\beta^{g}{}_{b]}{}^{c}-\beta^{Bc}{}_{[a}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{b]c}\right)

(125)

does not affect (
116
), because for
\beta^{g}=\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}
and
\beta^{B}=\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}
it vanishes.

4)

Eventually, we perform a scheme transformation from the MT scheme to the generalised Bergshoeff-de Roe scheme (gBdR). This transformation is required to bring the action
\widehat{S}^{(2)}
into an O(
D
,
D
)-covariant form
Marques:2015vua
. Thus, it is natural to apply it to
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
, too. In our conventions, this tranformation is parameterised by

\displaystyle\Delta^{(1)}g_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{ia}{}^{b}\omega_{jb}{}^{a}+\frac{3}{8}H^{2}_{ij}\,,

(126)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(1)}B_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\widehat{\beta}^{B(1)}_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}H_{[ia}{}^{b}\omega_{j]b}{}^{a}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\Delta^{(1)}d

\displaystyle=0

and implemented by the Lie derivative on the coupling space. We already discussed the latter for
\beta
-functions. Here, we extend it in the canonical way to
K^{(1)}
, namely

K^{(1)}(\Psi^{\prime},\upbeta)\rightarrow K^{(1)}(\Psi^{\prime},\upbeta)+L_{\Psi^{(1)}}K^{(0)}(\Psi^{\prime},\upbeta)

(127)

with

\displaystyle L_{\Psi}K^{(0)}(\Psi^{\prime},\upbeta)=

\displaystyle K^{(0)}(\delta_{\Psi^{\prime}}\Psi,\upbeta)+K^{(0)}(\Psi^{\prime},\delta_{\upbeta}\Psi)+

(128)

\displaystyle K^{(0)}(T(\Psi^{\prime},\Psi),\upbeta)+K^{(0)}(\Psi^{\prime},T(\upbeta,\Psi))\,,

where we understand
K
as a pairing between two vectors,

K(\Psi,\upbeta)=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\Psi\cdot K(\upbeta)\,,

(129)

on the infinite dimensional coupling space. Evaluating (
128
) for (
126
) is cumbersome, especially because (
126
) contains Lorentz symmetry violating terms. We approach this challenge by writing the one-loop
\beta
-functions in terms of the spin connection
\omega_{abc}
, the flat derivative
\widehat{D}_{a}
,
\widehat{F}_{a}
from (
32
) and the
H
-flux
H_{abc}
with the following, non-vanishing, variations

\displaystyle\delta_{\Psi}\omega_{abc}

\displaystyle=D_{[c}\delta g_{b]a}+\delta g_{d[b}\omega_{c]a}{}^{d}+\delta g_{ad}\omega_{[cb]}{}^{d}-\frac{1}{2}\delta g_{ad}\omega^{d}{}_{bc}\,,

(130)

\displaystyle\delta_{\Psi}H_{abc}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\delta g_{[a}{}^{d}H_{bc]d}+3\nabla_{[a}\delta B_{bc]}\,,

\displaystyle\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{F}_{a}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\widehat{D}_{a}\delta d+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\widehat{D}_{b}\delta g_{a}{}^{b}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{F}_{b}\delta g_{a}{}^{b}\qquad\text{and}\qquad

\displaystyle[\delta_{\Psi},\widehat{D}_{a}]=-\frac{1}{2}\delta g_{a}{}^{b}\widehat{D}_{b}\,.

To keep the following discussion more tractable, we split
K
into a symmetric and an antisymmetric part,

K_{\pm}(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\left[K(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\pm K(\Psi^{\prime},\Psi)\right]\,.

(131)

Most of
\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{+}
can be re-expressed in terms of O(
D
,
D
)-covariant quantities. Unfortunately, the situation for the asymmetric part is much worse. Hence, one might hope that there is a way to get rid of
\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{-}
and, while doing so, also to obtain the missing terms that are required to complete the doubling of
\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{+}
. Remarkably, this is indeed possible by applying a scheme transformation, which is linear in the one-loop
\beta
-functions, namely

\Delta^{(1)}g_{ij}=0\,,\qquad\Delta^{(1)}B_{ij}=\widehat{\beta}^{B(1)}_{ij}\,,\qquad\Delta^{(1)}d=0\,.

(132)

But instead of applying it to all quantities in (
98
), we only transform the
\beta
-functions

\widehat{\upbeta}^{\prime(2)}=\widehat{\upbeta}^{(2)}+L_{\Psi^{\prime}}\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)}\,.

(133)

While the first equation of (
98
) is not affected by this transformation, the second one becomes

\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\Psi\left[K^{(0)}(\widehat{\upbeta}^{\prime(2)})+\widehat{K}^{\prime(1)}(\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)})\right]

(134)

with

\widehat{K}^{\prime(1)}(\upbeta^{(1)})=\widehat{K}^{(1)}(\upbeta^{(1)})-K^{(0)}(L_{\Psi^{\prime}}\upbeta^{(1)})\,.

(135)

Here
\Psi^{\prime}
generates the scheme transformation (
132
) and, as intended, all terms of
K^{\prime(1)}_{-}
vanish. For the sake of brevity, we drop the prime from now on.

, representing the argument of

3)

\widehat{F}_{ABC}
,
\widehat{F}_{A}
and
\widehat{D}_{A}
contribute one derivative each. Thus, only two of them can be present in a diagram at a time. This results in six different combinations that we call types and number by roman numerals. Moreover, there are three different classes of diagrams where
\beta^{(1)E}_{a\bar{b}}
is connected to

A)

no external leg

B)

one external leg

C)

both external legs .

Finally, note that all diagrams have to come in pairs because
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
is even under the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry defined in (
61
). Hence, we only draw one diagram of each pair and understand that it has to be complemented by its partner, which arises under the swapping
P\leftrightarrow\overline{P}
. The resulting number of admissible diagrams for all types and the corresponding classes is summarised in table
1
. Going through this list, we find the factors listed in table
2
in front of the relevant diagrams by equating coefficients. At this point, we have to refine our prescription to construct the diagrams slightly because diagrams of type I have two derivatives acting on
\beta^{E}_{a\bar{b}}
. But these two derivatives do not commute and thus we have to decide which one comes first. Our convention is that we go from top to bottom and left to right. The order how we encounter derivatives is the order we write them down in the tensorial expression.

To avoid any confusion and since it is a main result of our work, the explicit tensor expression corresponding to the diagrams in table
2
reads

\displaystyle\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{AB}

\displaystyle(\upbeta)=-2P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}D_{E}D_{C}\upbeta_{DF}+2P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}D_{E}D_{D}\upbeta_{CF}

\displaystyle+2F_{CFH}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}D_{E}\upbeta_{DG}+2P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}\upbeta_{DG}D_{E}F_{CFH}

\displaystyle+2F_{EFH}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}D_{C}\upbeta_{DG}-2P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}P^{FG}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{H}\upbeta_{DH}D_{G}F_{CEF}

\displaystyle-2P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}\upbeta_{CG}D_{E}F_{DFH}-4F_{EFH}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}D_{D}\upbeta_{CG}

\displaystyle-4F_{CEG}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}P^{FG}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{H}D_{F}\upbeta_{DH}+2P_{[A}{}^{D}P^{CE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}F_{C}D_{D}\upbeta_{EF}

\displaystyle-2P_{[A}{}^{D}P^{CE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}F_{C}D_{E}\upbeta_{DF}+2F_{CHI}F_{EFJ}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}^{IJ}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}\upbeta_{DG}

\displaystyle-4F_{CEI}F_{FHJ}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}^{IJ}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}\upbeta_{DG}+F_{DGI}F_{FHJ}P_{[A}{}^{C}\overline{P}^{IJ}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{D}\overline{P}^{EF}\overline{P}^{GH}\upbeta_{CE}

\displaystyle-2F_{DFI}F_{EHJ}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}\overline{P}^{IJ}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}\upbeta_{CG}+F_{DFH}F_{EGJ}P_{[A}{}^{C}P^{DE}P^{FG}\overline{P}^{IJ}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{H}\upbeta_{CI}

\displaystyle-2F_{DFH}P_{[A}{}^{D}P^{CE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}F_{C}\upbeta_{EG}+4F_{EFH}P_{[A}{}^{D}P^{CE}\overline{P}_{B]}{}^{F}\overline{P}^{GH}F_{C}\upbeta_{DG}

\displaystyle-P\leftrightarrow\overline{P}\,.

(136)

3.2.3
Partial double Lorentz gauge fixing

There are still a few terms in
\widehat{K}^{(1)}(\upbeta)
which cannot be matched by the procedure above. However, we will now show that they are just an artifact of the partially double Lorentz fixed generalised frame field
\widehat{E}_{A}{}^{I}
used in the calculation. Its variation (
102
) contains the compensating double Lorentz transformation
\delta\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{gf}}_{AB}
and by restricting (
98
) to it, we find

\delta_{\Psi^{\mathrm{gf}}}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\delta\widehat{E}^{\mathrm{gf}}\cdot\widehat{K}^{(1)}(\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)})\,.

(137)

Since
\widehat{S}^{(2)}
is not invariant under double Lorentz transformations,
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
has to have contributions which relate physical degrees of freedom with gauge transformations. We fix them by remembering that
\widehat{S}^{(2)}
has been constructed such that the relation
Marques:2015vua

\delta_{\Psi^{\mathrm{gf}}}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}(A^{(1)}_{\Psi^{\mathrm{gf}}}\widehat{E})\widehat{\beta}^{(1)E}

(138)

holds. An analogous mechanism governs gauge fixed, two-loop
\beta
-functions, too. More precisely, they split into the two contributions

\widehat{\upbeta}^{E(2)}=\widehat{\upbeta}^{\prime E(2)}+A^{(1)}_{\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)E\mathrm{gf}}}\widehat{E}\,,

(139)

where
\widehat{\upbeta}^{\prime(1)E}
is not gauge fixed. In the final result, we neither want to include (
137
) nor the second term on the left hand side of (
139
). The reason is that we are looking for two-loop
\beta
-functions which do not depend on a particular gauge fixing. All terms that we therefore drop can be neatly combined in the symmetric, double Lorentz gauge fixing term

\widehat{K}^{(1)\mathrm{gf}}(\delta\widehat{E},\widehat{\upbeta})=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\left[-\frac{1}{4}\left(\delta g^{a}{}_{b}H^{bcd}-2\delta B^{a}{}_{b}\omega^{bcd}\right)D_{a}\widehat{\beta}^{B}_{cd}+\left(\widehat{\beta}^{E}\leftrightarrow\delta E\right)\right]

(140)

and we eventually find that
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
can be written as

\widehat{K}^{(1)}(\delta\widehat{E},\widehat{\upbeta})=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\delta\widehat{E}^{AB}\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{AB}(\widehat{\beta})+\widehat{K}^{(1)\mathrm{gf}}(\delta\widehat{E},\widehat{\beta})\,.

(141)

Note that we have dropped the prime on the
\widehat{\upbeta}^{\prime E}
to avoid cluttering our notation. From now on all doubled
\beta
-functions are free of any gauge fixing.

3.2.4
Extracting the
\beta
-functions

Since, we have been successful in writing
\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{AB}(\upbeta)
in the O(
D
,
D
)-covariant form (
3.2.2
), it is straightforward to compute the two-loop
\beta
-functions. The procedure goes along the same line as at one-loop in section
3.1
: First, we rewrite the gradient flow (
116
) in terms of doubled quantities. More specifically, we take the components of

\widehat{K}^{AB}(\upbeta)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\widehat{K}^{a\bar{b}}(\upbeta)\\
-\widehat{K}^{b\bar{a}}(\upbeta)&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\text{with}\qquad\widehat{K}^{a\bar{b}}(\upbeta)=\widehat{K}^{g\,a\bar{b}}(\upbeta)+\widehat{K}^{B\,a\bar{b}}(\upbeta)\,,

(142)

to rewrite (
116
) as

\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\left[\delta E^{AB}K^{(0)}_{AB}{}^{CD}\left(\widehat{\beta}^{(2)E}_{CD}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{CD}(\widehat{\upbeta})\right)+8\delta d\widehat{\beta}^{(2)d}\right]\,.

(143)

For the discussion in section
3.2.1
, we know that the action
\widehat{S}^{(2)}
has to be in the gBdR scheme to be compatible with our
\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{AB}
from section
3.2.2
. In this scheme, it can be written in the O(
D
,
D
)-covariant form
Marques:2015vua
;
Baron:2017dvb

\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int d^{D}xe^{-2d}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(2)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(2)}=-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{+}-\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{-}\,,

(144)

where the explicit expression for
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
is given in (2.33) of
Baron:2017dvb
. In terms of diagrams
\mathcal{R}^{(2)}
reads

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.58868}{-0.80836}{0.80836}{-0.58868}{19.11519pt}{5.58543pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.06 3.74 L 0.42 0 L -6.06 -3.74 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{{}}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope }\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}+P\leftrightarrow\overline{P}\,.\end{array}

(145)

All that is left to be done is compute the variation of this action. It has the form

\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\left(\delta E^{AB}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{(2)}_{AB}-2\delta d\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(2)}\right)

(146)

and immediately allows for the identification

\widehat{\beta}^{(2)E}_{a\bar{b}}=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{(2)}_{a\bar{b}}+\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{a\bar{b}}(\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)})\,,\qquad\widehat{\beta}^{(2)d}=-\frac{1}{4}\widehat{\mathcal{R}}^{(2)}\,.

(147)

We already computed
\widehat{K}^{(1)}_{a\bar{b}}
and therefore we only need
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}^{(2)}_{a\bar{b}}
to obtain the final result. We compute it with the xTensor package of the xAct suite and get the results presented in section
2.3
.

3.2.5
Generalised Green-Schwarz transformation

The last thing we have to do to make full contact with section
2.3
is to prove that (
65
) holds. To this end, we take a closer look at the identity

\delta_{\Psi}L_{\chi^{(1)}}\widehat{S}^{(1)}=L_{\chi^{(1)}}(\widehat{K}^{(0)})(\Psi,\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)})+\widehat{K}^{(0)}(\Psi,L_{\chi^{(1)}}\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)})\,,

(148)

which arises if we apply
L_{\chi}
to both sides of the first equation in (
98
). We now identify
\chi^{(1)}=A_{\lambda}^{(1)}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{E}&d\end{pmatrix}
to further simply this relation by using

L_{\chi^{(1)}}\widehat{S}^{(1)}=A_{\lambda}^{(0)}\widehat{S}^{(2)}\qquad\text{and}\quad L_{\chi^{(1)}}\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)}=A_{\lambda}^{(0)}\widehat{\upbeta}^{(2)}\,,

(149)

which are equivalent to (
64
) and (
65
), respectively. Together with (
148
) they imply

\delta_{\Psi}A_{\lambda}^{(0)}\widehat{S}^{(2)}=L_{\chi^{(1)}}(\widehat{K}^{(0)})(\Psi,\widehat{\upbeta}^{(1)})+\widehat{K}^{(0)}(\Psi,A_{\lambda}^{(0)}\widehat{\upbeta}^{(2)})=A_{\lambda}^{(0)}\delta_{\Psi}\widehat{S}^{(2)}\,.

(150)

Note that we are able to swap
\delta_{\Psi}
and
A_{\lambda}
because the variation parameter
\Psi
does not transform anomalously under double Lorentz transformations and therefore
A_{\lambda}\Psi=0
holds. This equation can be alternatively obtained by applying
A_{\lambda}^{(0)}
to the left and right side of the second equation of (
98
), if we further impose

L_{A_{\lambda}^{(1)}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{E}&d\end{pmatrix}}\widehat{K}^{(0)}=A_{\lambda}^{(0)}\widehat{K}^{(1)}\,.

(151)

Equally, one might conclude that if this identity holds for
\widehat{K}^{(0)}
and
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
, it implies (
65
). This result is not very surprising, because we expect
\widehat{K}
, like the action and the
\beta
-functions, to transform covariantly under gGS transformation. Indeed one can check that the expressions we have presented in (
106
) and (
3.2.2
) satisfy (
151
). This result provides an important consistency check. Moreover, it would be interesting to see if, similar to the action
\widehat{S}^{(2)}
,
\widehat{K}^{(1)}
can be completely fixed by just imposing its covariance under gGS transformations.

3.3
c
-function and gradient flow metric

We argue in section
2.2.2
that PL symmetry restricts the
\sigma
-model
\beta
-functions to a finite dimensional subspace of the coupling space. The same is true for
K_{AB}(\upbeta)
, which looses all derivatives on a PL symmetric background and thus can be written as

K(\Psi,\upbeta)=\delta E_{AB}\beta^{E}_{CD}K^{ABCD}V\,,\qquad\text{with}\qquad V=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\,.

(152)

Here,
K^{ABCD}
only depends on the couplings that enter through
F_{ABC}
and
F_{A}
. In the same vein, we rewrite the low-energy effective target space action,

S=V\mathcal{R}=-4V\beta^{d}=-\frac{2}{3}Vc\,,

(153)

where the last identity originates from (
81
). Now, the gradient flow (
97
) takes a form that matches (14) in Zamolodchikov’s famous paper
Zamolodchikov:1986gt
, namely

\partial_{\nu}c=12G_{\mu\nu}\beta^{\nu}

(154)

with the gradient flow metric

G_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{8}J_{\mu}{}^{AB}J_{\nu}{}^{CD}K_{ABCD}\,,

(155)

and the Jacobian

J_{\mu}{}^{AB}=\partial_{\mu}E^{AI}E^{B}{}_{I}\,.

(156)

Because
K^{(n)}_{ABCD}
is symmetric under the exchange of the indices
AB\leftrightarrow CD
,
G^{(n)}_{\mu\nu}
is a symmetric tensor, at least for
n=0\,,1
. Hence, one might conclude that the latter is the Zamolodchikov metric
Zamolodchikov:1986gt
. But the gradient flow away from the conformal point has a more general form and incorporates corrections
Friedan:2009ik
. Therefore, we prefer the term gradient flow metric for
G_{\mu\nu}
. On the other hand, the action
S
in (
153
) has the “central charge” form of
Tseytlin:1987bz
and thus, what we call
c
should match Zamolodchikov’s definition. A thorough comparison between the quantities, we identified here, and results from the fixed point CFT and its conformal perturbation theory is required to settle these points completely. This analysis is beyond the scope of this paper. But as a first step, we discuss the
\lambda
-deformation in the following, which was extensively studied from a CFT perspective
Itsios:2014lca
;
Georgiou:2016iom
.

3.3.1
\lambda
-deformation

We already have computed
c
of the
\lambda
- and
\eta
-deformation for one and two loops in the sections
2.2.3
and
2.3.5
, respectively. For convenience, we repeat it here,

c=D-\frac{1+2\lambda+2\lambda^{3}+\lambda^{4}}{2k(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)^{3}}c_{G}D+\frac{\lambda^{3}(4-5\lambda+4\lambda^{2})}{2k^{2}(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)^{6}}c_{G}^{2}D\,,

(157)

in terms of
\lambda
instead of
\kappa
. While the first two terms match (3.30) of
Georgiou:2019nbz
perfectly, the last term deviates. A possible explanation is that our
c
and theirs actually capture different quantities. The derivation of
c
in
Georgiou:2019nbz
starts from the Zamolodchikov metric, obtained by conformal perturbation theory. Combining the Zamolodchikov metric and the
\beta
-functions,
\partial_{\mu}c
is calculated and then integrated to obtain
c
. As explained above, our
G_{\mu\nu}
is expected to differ from the Zamolodchikov metric away from the conformal point.

Because there is only one coupling that flows,
G_{\mu\nu}
is solely formed by
G_{\lambda\lambda}
. Evaluating (
155
) with

J_{\lambda}{}^{AB}=\frac{1}{1-\lambda^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta_{a\bar{b}}\\
-\eta_{b\bar{a}}&0\end{pmatrix}

(158)

works along the same line as for the
\beta
-functions. We will not repeat the details here but instead refer to the accompanying Mathematica notebook
PLtwoloop.nb
. The result

G_{\lambda\lambda}=\frac{D}{2(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}}\left(1+\frac{Q(\lambda)}{k(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)^{3}}c_{G}\right)\,.

(159)

matches (3.16) of
Georgiou:2019nbz
. There it is argued that the function
Q
have to have the form

Q(\lambda)=c_{0}+c_{1}\lambda+c_{2}\lambda^{2}+c_{1}\lambda^{3}+c_{0}\lambda^{4}

(160)

to be compatible with the symmetry
\lambda\leftrightarrow\lambda^{-1}
,
k\leftrightarrow-k
. We find a
Q(\lambda)
of this form, but instead of
c_{0}=c_{1}=c_{2}=0

Georgiou:2019nbz
, we obtain

c_{0}=-1\,,\qquad c_{1}=2\,,\quad\text{and}\quad c_{2}=-4\,.

(161)

This is not very surprising because already our
c
-function is different from theirs.

It should be possible to better understand this discrepancy by using alternative techniques to obtain the values of these coefficients. In particular,
c_{0}
is accessible from the level
\widehat{k}
WZW-model on the group manifold
G
, which arises at the fixed point
\lambda=0
of the RG flow. At this distinguished point, the marginal operator that triggers the flow is

\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(z,\overline{z})=\frac{\gamma}{k}\eta_{a\bar{b}}\,j^{a}(z)\overline{j}^{\bar{b}}(\overline{z})\,,

(162)

where
\gamma
is a numerical factor. Most important is that
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
is proportional to
k^{-1}
and not
\widehat{k}
. This dependence enters through the left and right invariant forms (
14
). The Kač-Moody currents, which
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
is formed of, are governed by the OPE

j^{a}(z)j^{b}(w)=\frac{\widehat{k}\eta^{ab}}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{f^{ab}{}_{c}}{z-w}j^{c}(w)+\dots\,.

(163)

The anti-chiral currents
\overline{j}^{a}(\overline{z})
are governed by an analogous version. Moreover, they commute with all chiral currents
j^{b}(z)
. We now know everything we need to compute the Zamolodchikov metric

G_{\lambda\lambda}(0)=\lim_{z\rightarrow w}|z-w|^{4}\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(z,\overline{z})\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}(w,\overline{w})\right\rangle=\gamma^{2}D\frac{\widehat{k}^{2}}{k^{2}}=\gamma^{2}D\left(1-\frac{c_{G}}{k}+\frac{c_{G}^{2}}{4k^{2}}\right)

(164)

from (6c) in
Zamolodchikov:1986gt
. Matching this result with (
159
), we recover
c_{0}=-1
and furthermore fix
\gamma^{2}=1/2
. This is consistent with the observation that, at least at the fixed point, additional corrections
Friedan:2009ik
vanish and therefore
G_{\lambda\lambda}(\lambda=0)
becomes the Zamolodchikov metric. The difference to
c_{0}=0
in
Georgiou:2019nbz
originates from a different normalisation of
\mathcal{O}_{\lambda}
, since they use
\hat{k}
instead of
k
in (
162
)
7
7

7

We thank the authors of
Georgiou:2019nbz
for explaining to use their calculations.
. Clearly, more work is required to understand this difference and to try to reproduces the remaining two coefficients,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, from conformal perturbation theory.

4
Conclusion

In this paper, we have established three main results for the bosonic string:

1)

In an appropriate scheme, the two-loop
\beta
-functions for the metric,
B
-field, and dilaton can be written in a manifestly O(
D
,
D
)-covariant form.

2)

PL
\sigma
-models are one and two-loop renormalisable.

3)

The respective RG flows are invariant under PL T-duality.

One might expect that the best way to obtain them is to start from a worldsheet theory with manifest, classical PL symmetry and apply the background field method like in
Sfetsos:2009vt
. However, this idea has not been implemented successfully yet. Therefore, we chose a different approach which heavily relies on previous insights in DFT and on the option to obtain the one and two-loop
\beta
-functions from a gradient flow. An important lesson learned is that it is crucial to work in the right scheme. The latter is tightly linked to the deformation of double Lorentz symmetry on the target space and the corresponding gGS transformations. So it might be promising to revisit the worldsheet approach with this knowledge.

The one-loop RG flow has a natural interpretation in terms of a generalised Ricci flow (see
Garcia-Fernandez:2020ope
for a recent review), the generalised geometry version of the celebrated Ricci flow
hamilton1982
used in Perelman’s resolution of the Poincaré and Thurston geometrisation conjecture
Perelman:2006un
. Therefore, all involved quantities possess a (generalised) geometric origin. It is tempting to speculate that something similar might be true for the two-loop flow. Since fundamental symmetries of generalised geometry (like double Lorentz transformations) are deformed in its derivation, it is likely that also the underlying notion of geometry has to be adapted. PL symmetric target space geometries provide intriguing clues on the required modifications: Remember that a significant class of such target spaces is formed by PL groups. But PL groups are just the classical limit of a quantum group (see for example
Chaichian:1996ah
for an introduction). Quantum groups can be approached from different angles. Most significant for us is that they give rise to non-commutative geometries. Hence, we conjecture that
\beta
-functions beyond one-loop might be governed by non-commutative geometry where the deformation parameter is related to the string length
\sim\sqrt{\alpha}^{\prime}
. A related clue in this direction is that integrable deformations, like the
\lambda
- and
\eta
-deformation, which we discuss in section
2
, possess a hidden quantum group symmetry
Hollowood:2015dpa
;
Delduc:2016ihq
. The respective deformations parameters,
q=\exp(i\pi/k)
and
q=\exp(4\pi\eta t)
, are RG invariants at one and two loops. It is instructive to restore
\alpha^{\prime}
in these expressions. We know that
F_{ABC}
comes with one derivative and therefore a factor of
\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Hence, we are actually dealing with
\widehat{q}=q^{\alpha^{\prime}}
. In the semiclassical limit,
\alpha^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
, a
\widehat{q}
deformed quantum group transitions into a Poisson-Hopf algebra with the deformation parameter
q
. It is the latter which partially captures the global symmetries of the classical
\eta
-deformation
Delduc:2016ihq
. Consequentially, we might understand
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrections as the driving force from the classical Poisson-Hopf algebra to the associated quantum group. Of course, these speculations have to be supplemented with further quantitative evidence. But if we assume that they are justified, it would imply that we could extract all order
\beta
-functions and their generating low-energy, effective target space actions. Another reason to be optimistic that our results can be extended beyond two loops is that gGS transformations and the corresponding O(
D
,
D
)-covariant action are in principle (even though they become extremely complicated) available to arbitrary order in
\alpha^{\prime}

Baron:2020xel
.

Two immediate applications for our results are integrable deformations and consistent truncations with higher derivative corrections. The former are motivated by the observation that nearly all currently known integrable
\sigma
-models possess PL symmetry. Already at one-loop, they have interesting RG flows (examples include
Delduc:2020vxy
) with generic features like multiple fixed points
Georgiou:2020eoo
. Recently, first efforts were made to push this analysis to two-loop
Hoare:2019ark
;
Hoare:2019mcc
;
Georgiou:2019nbz
. At the level of the target space fields this is challenging, as we have demonstrated in section
2.4
for the
\lambda
-deformation. But with the formalism we develop in this paper, it becomes a much simpler task. Moreover, PL T-dualities between different integrable deformations are manifest. Due to this fact, we could obtain the flows of the
\lambda
- and
\eta
-deformation from a single calculation. We furthermore noticed that at one-loop, renormalisable
\sigma
-models are in one-to-one correspondence with consistent truncations of the low energy effective theory in the target space. Due to their potential to produce new, sophisticated solutions in (gauged) supergravity they have been intensively studied (for an early work see for example
Duff:1985jd
). But only recently, systematic constructions of such truncations have been discussed and the framework of generalised geometry/double/exceptional field theory is predestined for them
Cassani:2019vcl
. All of the work in this direction, that we are aware of, is based on a two-derivative action and its field equations. Since PL
\sigma
-models are two-loop renormalisable, they result in a large class of consistent truncations involving up to four derivatives. Hence, one might use them as guiding examples to construct a higher derivative version of the current constructions. Another important step that is required to make contact with
\alpha^{\prime}
-corrected half-maximal gauged supergravities, is to extend our results to the heterotic string.

Appendix A
Two-loop
\beta
-functions

In this appendix we demonstrate how the two-loop
\beta
-functions arise from the variation of the two-loop low-energy effective target space action in the MT scheme. We follow the presentation of
Metsaev:1987zx
closely but keep the one-loop
\beta
-functions in all steps instead of setting them to zero.

A.1
Metric

We begin by varying the two-loop low-energy effective target space action (
115
) with respect to the metric. As explained in section
3.1
, it is important that one does not vary with respect to the inverse metric, as this would introduce a wrong sign. For instance, the variation of the Riemann tensor is given by

\delta R^{e}{}_{abc}=\frac{1}{2}g^{de}\left(\nabla_{b}\delta\left(\partial_{a}g_{dc}+\partial_{c}g_{da}-\partial_{l}g_{ca}\right)-\nabla_{c}\delta\left(\partial_{a}g_{db}+\partial_{b}g_{da}-\partial_{d}g_{ba}\right)\right),

(165)

so that the variation of the first term in (
115
) reads

\begin{split}\delta\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}e^{-2\phi}\frac{1}{4}R_{efcd}R^{efcd}&=-\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}e^{-2\phi}R^{abcd}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{c}\delta g_{bd}\\
&=-\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{c}\left(e^{-2\phi}R^{abcd}\right)\delta g_{bd}\\
&=-\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}\left(2R^{acdb}\Big(\nabla_{c}\partial_{d}\phi-2\partial_{c}\phi\partial_{d}\phi\right)\\
&\hskip 50.00008pt+4\nabla_{c}R^{c(ab)d}\partial_{d}\phi-\nabla_{c}\nabla_{d}R^{acdb}\Big)\delta g_{ab},\end{split}

(166)

where we integrated by parts twice in the second line. Following
Metsaev:1987zx
, we break down the metric variation of (
115
) into the three terms

\displaystyle P_{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv e^{2\phi}\frac{\delta}{\delta g^{ab}}\int\frac{1}{4}e^{-2\phi}R_{cdef}R^{cdef},

(167)

\displaystyle Q_{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv e^{2\phi}\frac{\delta}{\delta g^{ab}}\int\left(-\frac{1}{8}\right)e^{-2\phi}R^{cdef}H_{cdh}H_{ef}{}^{h},

(168)

\displaystyle O_{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta}{\delta g^{ab}}\int\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{24}H_{cde}H^{d}{}_{fg}H^{fhe}H_{h}{}^{cg}-\frac{1}{8}H_{cde}H_{f}{}^{de}H^{cgh}H^{f}{}_{gh}\right),

(169)

where we have already partially treated the first term in (
166
). We have to apply Bianchi identities to further simplify these three contributions. In particular, starting from the second Bianchi identity of the Riemann tensor, we derive the identity

\nabla^{c}\nabla^{d}R_{acdb}=-\nabla^{2}R_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}R+R_{acdb}R^{cd}+R_{ac}R^{c}{}_{b}.

(170)

Applying it to the first term, we obtain

\begin{split}P_{ab}=&-\frac{1}{2}R_{acde}R_{b}{}^{cde}-\nabla^{2}R_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}R+R_{acdb}R^{cd}+R_{ac}R^{c}{}_{b}\\
&-4\left(\nabla_{(a}R_{b)c}-\nabla_{c}R_{ab}\right)\partial^{c}\phi-2R_{acdb}\left(\nabla^{c}\partial^{d}\phi-2\partial^{c}\phi\partial^{d}\phi\right).\end{split}

(171)

Similarly, the second and third terms reduce to

\displaystyle Q_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}R^{cdef}H_{acd}H_{bef}+\frac{3}{2}R^{cde}{}_{(a}H_{b)ge}H_{cd}{}^{g}+e^{2\phi}\nabla^{c}\nabla^{d}\left(e^{-2\phi}H_{ace}H_{bd}{}^{e}\right),

(172)

\displaystyle O_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16}(H^{4})_{ab}+\frac{1}{16}H^{2}_{ac}(H^{2})_{b}{}^{c}+\frac{1}{8}(H^{2})^{cd}H_{ace}H_{bd}{}^{e}.

(173)

As suggested by
Metsaev:1987zx
, we use the one-loop
\beta
-functions to remove all
\phi
-dependence. Consequentially, the variation of the action decomposed into terms containing
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}
or
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b}
and terms without them. The latter form the two-loop
\beta
-function for the metric,
\widehat{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}
, while the former give rise to
\widehat{K}^{(1)g}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g},\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})
. During the computation we use the identities

\displaystyle R_{acdb}\nabla^{c}\phi=2\nabla_{[c}\nabla_{b]}\nabla_{a}\phi=\nabla_{[d}\left(\widehat{\beta}^{g}_{b]a}-R_{b]a}+\frac{1}{4}H^{2}_{b]a}\right),

(174)

\displaystyle\nabla^{c}\nabla_{a}R_{cb}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}R+R_{ac}R^{c}{}_{b}+R_{acdb}R^{cd},

\displaystyle\nabla_{b}H_{acd}=\nabla_{c}H_{bda}+\nabla_{d}H_{bac}+\nabla_{a}H_{bcd}\,,

which eventually yield

\begin{split}P_{ab}=&-\frac{1}{2}R_{acde}R_{b}{}^{cde}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{e}H_{acd}\nabla^{e}H_{b}{}^{cd}-\frac{1}{16}(H^{2})_{a}{}^{c}H^{2}_{cb}-\frac{1}{4}H_{cea}H_{bd}{}^{e}(H^{2})^{cd}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}R^{cdef}H_{acd}H_{bef}+\frac{1}{4}R_{acdb}(H^{2})^{cd}+R_{(a}{}^{cde}H_{b)cf}H_{de}{}^{f}+\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}H^{2}\\
&-\nabla^{2}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{ab}+\nabla^{c}\nabla_{(a}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{b)c}-H^{cd}{}_{a}H_{edb}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g})_{c}{}^{e}-\frac{1}{4}H^{2}_{ac}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g})_{b}{}^{c}-2\left(\nabla_{(a}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{b)c}-\nabla_{c}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{ab}\right)\partial^{c}\phi\\
&+2H^{cd}{}_{a}\nabla_{c}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b}_{db}+\frac{1}{2}H^{cd}{}_{a}\nabla_{b}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b}_{cd}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{(a}H_{b)cd}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b})^{cd},\\
Q_{ab}=&\frac{1}{4}R_{(a}{}^{cde}H_{b)cf}H_{de}{}^{f}+\frac{1}{16}H_{ace}H_{bd}{}^{e}(H^{2})^{cd}+\frac{1}{8}\nabla_{e}H_{acd}\nabla^{e}H_{b}{}^{cd}-\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{a}H_{cde}\nabla_{b}H^{cde}\\
&+\frac{1}{4}H_{ace}H_{bd}{}^{e}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g})^{dc}-\nabla^{c}\widehat{\beta}_{d(a}^{(1)b}H_{b)c}{}^{d}+(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b})_{ca}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b})^{c}{}_{b},\\
O_{ab}=&-\frac{1}{16}(H^{4})_{ab}+\frac{1}{16}H^{2}_{ac}(H^{2})_{b}{}^{c}+\frac{1}{8}(H^{2})^{cd}H_{ace}H_{bd}{}^{e}\,.\end{split}

(175)

Adding those terms back together gives rise to

P_{ab}+Q_{ab}+O_{ab}=-\widehat{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}+\widehat{K}^{(1)g}_{ab}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g},\widehat{\beta}^{(1)b})\,.

(176)

Now, we read off
\widehat{K}^{(1)g}_{ab}(\beta^{g},\beta^{B})
in (
118
) and

\widehat{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}=\overline{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}-\frac{1}{24}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}H^{2}\,.

(177)

A.2
B
-field

Since the
B
-field only appears indirectly through
H=dB
(
H_{abc}=3\partial_{[a}B_{bc]}
),
8
8

8

Due to the total antisymmetrisation we can equivalently write
H_{abc}=3\nabla_{[a}B_{bc]}
.

we vary the action (
115
) with respect to
H
, apply the chain-rule and integrate by parts

\delta\widehat{S}^{(2)}=-3\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}\nabla_{c}\frac{\delta\widehat{L}^{(2)}}{\delta H_{cab}}\delta B_{ab}\,,\qquad\text{where}\qquad\widehat{S}^{(2)}=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}\widehat{L}^{(2)}\,.

(178)

The result reads

\begin{split}\frac{\delta\widehat{S}^{(2)}}{\delta B^{ab}}=&\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{e}\left(e^{-2\phi}\left(R_{ab}{}^{cd}H_{cde}+R_{ea}{}^{cd}H_{cdb}+R_{be}{}^{cd}H_{cda}\right)\right)\\
&-\frac{1}{8}\nabla^{f}\left(e^{-2\phi}H_{acd}H_{be}{}^{c}H_{f}{}^{ed}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{8}\nabla^{f}\left(e^{-2\phi}\left(H_{abc}H_{def}H^{dec}+H_{fac}H_{deb}H^{dec}+H_{bfc}H_{dea}H^{dec}\right)\right)\\
=&\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}e^{-2\phi}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{e}\phi\left(R_{ab}{}^{cd}H_{cde}+R_{ea}{}^{cd}H_{cdb}+R_{be}{}^{cd}H_{cda}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{f}\phi H_{acd}H_{be}{}^{c}H_{f}{}^{ed}\\
&-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{f}\phi\left(H_{abc}H_{def}H^{dec}+H_{fac}H_{deb}H^{dec}+H_{bfc}H_{dea}H^{dec}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{e}\left(R_{ab}{}^{cd}H_{cde}+R_{ea}{}^{cd}H_{cdb}+R_{be}{}^{cd}H_{cda}\right)-\frac{1}{8}\nabla^{f}\left(H_{acd}H_{be}{}^{c}H_{f}{}^{ed}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{8}\nabla^{f}\left(H_{abc}H_{def}H^{dec}+H_{fac}H_{deb}H^{dec}+H_{bfc}H_{dea}H^{dec}\right)\Big]\,.\end{split}

(179)

Again all terms containing the dilaton can be eliminated in favour of
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}
and
\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}
, yielding

\begin{split}\frac{\delta\widehat{S}^{(2)}}{\delta B^{ab}}&=\int\mathrm{d}^{D}x\sqrt{g}e^{-2\phi}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}R_{[aecd}\nabla^{e}H^{cd}{}_{b]}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{f}H_{cd[a}H_{b]e}{}^{c}H_{f}{}^{ed}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{c}H^{2}_{d[b}H_{a]}{}^{dc}\\
&+\frac{1}{8}H^{2}_{ec}\nabla^{e}H^{c}{}_{ab}+\frac{1}{8}H_{abc}H_{fde}\nabla^{f}H^{dec}-H_{[a|}{}^{cd}\nabla_{c}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)g}_{d|b]}-\frac{1}{2}R_{ab}{}^{cd}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{cd}\\
&-\frac{1}{4}H_{abc}H^{dec}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{de}+\frac{1}{4}H_{acd}H_{be}{}^{c}(\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B})^{ed}-\frac{1}{2}H_{de[a|}H^{dec}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{c|b]}\Big]\,,\end{split}

(180)

from which we read off
\widehat{K}^{(1)B}_{ab}(\beta^{g},\beta^{B})
in (
119
) and

\widehat{\beta}^{(2)B\,ab}=\overline{\beta}^{(2)B}-\frac{1}{48}H_{ab}{}^{c}\nabla_{c}H^{2}\,.

(181)

A.3
Dilaton

Finally, for the dilaton, we begin with the two-loop
\beta
-function in the MT scheme, given in equation (6.10) of
Metsaev:1987zx
, namely

\widehat{\beta}^{(2)\phi}=\overline{\beta}^{(2)\phi}-\frac{1}{48}\nabla^{c}\phi\nabla_{c}H^{2}\,.

(182)

Combined with the
\beta
-function of the metric, it gives rise to
9
9

9

We make use of the Bianchi identity
\nabla^{2}H^{2}=6R^{ab}(H^{2})_{ab}-6RHH+2\nabla_{d}H_{abc}\nabla^{d}H^{abc}+6H_{abc}\nabla^{c}\nabla_{l}H^{lab}
in the step before last.

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(2)d}=

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}^{(2)\phi}-\frac{1}{4}g^{ab}\widehat{\beta}^{(2)g}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16}\Big(R_{abcd}R^{abcd}+\frac{1}{24}H^{4}+\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{d}H_{abc}\nabla^{d}H^{abc}-\frac{1}{8}H_{ab}^{2}(H^{2})^{ab}

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}RHH+R^{ab}H^{2}_{ab}-\frac{1}{6}\nabla^{2}H^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\nabla^{c}\phi\nabla_{c}H^{2}+2H^{2}_{ab}\nabla^{a}\nabla^{b}\phi\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16}\Big(R_{abcd}R^{abcd}+\frac{1}{24}H^{4}-\frac{1}{8}H_{ab}^{2}(H^{2})^{ab}-\frac{1}{2}RHH-H_{abc}\nabla^{c}\nabla_{l}H^{l}{}_{ab}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}\nabla^{c}\phi\nabla_{c}H^{2}+2H^{2}_{ab}\nabla^{a}\nabla^{b}\phi\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16}\Big(R_{abcd}R^{abcd}+\frac{1}{24}H^{4}-\frac{1}{8}H_{ab}^{2}(H^{2})^{ab}-\frac{1}{2}RHH+2H^{abc}\nabla_{c}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ab}\Big)\,,

(183)

where in the last step, we absorbed the terms involving
\phi
into the one-loop
\beta
-function of the
B
-field. Moreover, the variation of the action (
115
), with respect to the dilaton is given

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S^{(2)}}{\delta d}=-2\int\mathrm{d}^{D}xe^{-2d}\frac{1}{4}\Big[

\displaystyle R_{abcd}R^{abcd}-\frac{1}{2}R^{abcd}H_{abe}H_{cd}{}^{e}

(184)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{24}H_{abc}H^{b}{}_{de}H^{dfc}H_{f}{}^{ae}-\frac{1}{8}H_{abc}H_{d}{}^{bc}H^{aef}H^{d}{}_{ef}\Big]\,.

Combining it with (
116
), we read off the value of
\widehat{K}^{(1)d}(\beta^{B})
given in (
117
).

Appendix B
Transformation from HT to the MT scheme

Starting from the two-loop
\beta
-function of the
B
-field in the HT scheme, we show the details of the scheme transformations required to obtain the corresponding
\beta
-function in the MT scheme. Our main motivation for this calculation is to have a cross check for (
113
), because it deviates by two signs from
Metsaev:1987zx
.
\beta
-functions in both schemes are in general related by

\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{MT}}_{ij}=\widehat{\beta}^{\mathrm{HT}}_{ij}-\Delta\widehat{\beta}_{ij}\,,

(185)

and the metric is shifted by
Metsaev:1987zx

\Delta g^{(1)}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}H_{ij}^{2}\,,

(186)

while the
B
-field and the dilaton are not affected. Accordingly, the
B
-field
\beta
-function is shifted by

\Delta\widehat{\beta}^{(2)B}=\Delta g^{(1)}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta g}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}\,.

(187)

Explicitly calculating the variation with respect to the metric on the right hand side yields

\delta_{g}\widehat{\beta}^{(1)B}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}H_{ij}{}^{k}\nabla^{l}\delta g_{kl}+H_{[i}{}^{kl}\nabla_{k}\delta g_{j]l}-\frac{1}{4}g^{kl}H_{ij}{}^{n}\nabla_{n}\delta g_{kl}+\frac{1}{2}\delta g_{lk}\nabla^{l}H^{k}{}_{ij}-\delta g_{lk}\nabla^{l}\phi H^{k}{}_{ij}\,.

(188)

and thus

\Delta\widehat{\beta}^{(2)B}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{k}H^{2}_{l[j}H_{i]}{}^{kl}+\frac{1}{4}H^{2}_{lk}\nabla^{l}H^{k}{}_{ij}+H_{ij}{}^{k}\xi_{k}

(189)

with

\xi_{k}=\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{l}H^{2}_{lk}-\frac{1}{8}\nabla_{k}H^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{l}\phi H^{2}_{lk}\,.

(190)

Since that last term in (
189
) just generates an infinitesimal diffeomorphism, we can drop it when computing
\overline{\beta}^{(2)B\,\mathrm{MT}}_{ab}
from the expression in the HT scheme,

\overline{\beta}^{(2)B\,\mathrm{HT}}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{k}H^{lm}{}_{[j}R_{i]klm}-\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{l}H^{km}{}_{[j}H_{i]kn}H_{lm}{}^{n}+\frac{1}{8}\nabla_{k}H_{lij}(H^{2})^{kl}\,.

(191)

The result

\overline{\beta}^{(2)B\,\mathrm{MT}}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}R_{[i|klm}\nabla^{k}H^{lm}{}_{|j]}+\frac{1}{4}\nabla^{l}H_{mn[i}H_{j]k}{}^{m}H_{l}{}^{kn}-\frac{1}{8}H^{2}_{kl}\nabla^{k}H^{l}{}_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}H_{[i}{}^{kl}\nabla_{k}H^{2}_{j]l}\,.

(192)

matches (
113
) and confirms our result from appendix
A.2
.

Appendix C
Mathematica notebook

We include in the arXiv submission of this paper the Mathematica notebook
PLtwoloop.nb
. It contains equations (
37
), (
38
), (
69
), (
72
), (
106
) and (
3.2.2
) in a machine readable form. To demonstrate how to use them, this notebook further demonstrates detailed computations for the SU(2)
\lambda
- and
\eta
-deformation along the lines of sections
2.2.3
,
2.3.5
and
3.3.1
.

There are two ways to access
PLtwoloop.nb
: It can be downloaded directly from

https://fhassler.de/files/PLtwoloop.nb
.

Alternatively, one can download the source of the arXiv submission from the arXiv website. It is typically a .tar.gz archive and has to be extracted with an appropriate program.
